People are deeply pessimistic about AI’s impact on
jobs in general, yet relatively few expect their own
job to disappear. The bigger concern is how tasks will
change and who will be able to adapt (Figure 10). Net
sentiment towards AI’s impact on jobs stands at -67%,
the most negative reading on AI attitudes in the survey.
However, only 7% of respondents expect AI to replace
their own job entirely within the next five years. A further

21% expect significant parts of their work to be replaced,
while 26% expect AI mainly to assist and augment what
they do. Another 25% foresee little or no impact, while
20% do not know. The apparent paradox between very
negative views of AI’s overall impact on employment
and relatively limited fears of personal job loss can
therefore be reconciled: people expect AI to reshape
tasks and job content even if they do not expect mass
unemployment (see our forecast on the macroeconomic

and labor market impact of AI in Box 2).
Across countries, the main difference is not whether
people expect change, but whether they see AI primarily
as a substitute, a complement or an unknown. The US
records the highest expectation of outright displacement:
12% expect their job to disappear entirely, almost twice
the European average, and another 21% expect significant
parts to be replaced. Spain also anticipates substantial
change, with 25% expecting significant task replacement,

but this is matched by the highest share expecting AI to
assist their work (31%, alongside Austria) and the lowest
level of uncertainty in the sample at just 13%. Austria
similarly leans towards augmentation, with 31% expecting
AI to assist and support them. France and Germany, by
contrast, stand out less for replacement fears than for
uncertainty: around one in four respondents do not know
how AI will affect their job. These differences suggest
that labor-market anxiety has different forms across

countries: substitution fears are more pronounced in the
US, expectations of task reconfiguration are strongest in
Spain, while parts of Europe remain unsure about what the
transition will mean in practice.
Jobs: rising concern, but task
disruption rather than job
destruction

Expectations of both augmentation and automation

due to AI are highest among younger and highly-
educated respondents, the same groups that are also
more exposed to the technology in their daily lives and
work. Some 43% of Gen Z expect AI to replace their job
either entirely or in significant part, compared with just
14% of Boomers. At the same time, 34% of Gen Z expect
AI mainly to assist them at work, more than twice the
share among Boomers (15%), while only 11% say they do
not know what AI will mean for their job, compared with

34% of Boomers. A similar pattern emerges by education.
Almost one-third of tertiary-educated respondents
expect full or significant replacement (32%), compared
with 28% among those with primary education, but
they are also almost three times as likely to expect
augmentation (33% versus 12%) and far less likely to be
uncertain (14% versus 36%). Income differences point
in the same direction: high-income respondents are
somewhat more likely than low-income respondents to

expect full or significant replacement (34% versus 29%),
but also more likely to expect AI to assist them (26% versus
17%), while uncertainty is much higher among low-income
respondents (29% versus 17%). The divide is therefore not
simply between groups that expect disruption and those
that do not: the groups most exposed to AI tend to expect
more change in both directions, while less exposed groups
are much more uncertain about what the technology will

mean for them.

### Page 17

14 October 2026
17
To assess whether direct exposure to AI changes
how people expect it to affect their jobs, we split
respondents into three mutually exclusive groups
according to how they currently use the technology.
Around one-third already use AI at work (33%),
comprising both those who use it mainly at work
and equally at work and at home, while 37% use it
only privately and 30% do not use AI at all. We then
compare expectations for AI’s impact on their own

job across these three groups, using the full sample
as a benchmark. This allows us to distinguish whether
greater exposure is associated primarily with fears of
replacement, expectations of augmentation or simply a
clearer view of how work is likely to change.
The more people use AI at work, the more job
transformation they expect, but exposure raises
expectations of augmentation even more strongly
than fears of replacement (Figure 11). Among AI users

at work, 40% expect AI to replace their job entirely or
take over significant parts of it, compared with just 17%
of non-users. However, the gap is even larger on the
positive side: 41% of workplace users expect AI mainly to
assist and support them, almost four times the 11% share
among non-users. This asymmetry fits with our earlier
finding that people who already use AI tend to view the
technology more positively. Exposure appears to make
both the disruptive and complementary potential of AI

more tangible, but it is especially the expected upside
that is missing among non-users.
The other striking gradient is uncertainty, which falls
sharply as exposure to AI increases. Only 11% expect
augmentation, while 33% foresee little or no impact and
another 39% simply do not know what AI will mean for
their job. Together, almost three-quarters of non-users
therefore either expect no meaningful effect or cannot yet
assess one, compared with just 19% of workplace users.

Private-only users again occupy the middle ground: 27%
expect full or significant replacement, 26% augmentation,
30% little or no impact and 18% remain uncertain. The
gradient suggests that direct experience shifts perceptions
away from indifference and uncertainty towards a
much more concrete view of AI as both a source of task
substitution and a tool for productive support. AI users
have thus a much clearer view of how the technology
could change their work, seeing both greater disruption

and substantially greater potential for augmentation.

### Page 18

Allianz Research
AI is likely to reshape far more jobs than it destroys, but the scale of labor-market adjustment will still be
substantial. Our estimates suggest that AI could affect 23% of jobs across the major advanced economies over the next
one to three years³.  The estimate is built bottom-up from more than 800 occupations, which we classify according to
four possible AI impacts: no exposure, automation, augmentation or reorganization. We aggregate these occupation-

level effects into sectoral AI-exposure scores and then to the country level, taking into account sectoral employment
shares as well as structural factors such as AI adoption and preparedness, labor-market regulation and unit labor
costs. Job reorganization is the largest channel, accounting for 10% of jobs, as workers remain in their roles but AI takes
over or changes some of their tasks. A further 8% of jobs face a higher risk of automation, while 5% could benefit from

augmentation. Exposure across countries differs markedly, largely because of differences in their sectoral composition
as well as structural factors, such as labor market regulations and the institutional context. In the US, where finance,
ICT and other knowledge-intensive services account for a larger share of employment, 29% of jobs could be affected,
compared with 15% across the major European economies. Within Europe, the share ranges from 18% in the UK and 16%

in Germany to 14% in France, 12% in Spain and just 9% in Italy. The US therefore faces greater near-term disruption, but is
also better-positioned to capture the productivity and employment gains from AI.
The first effects are likely to appear in hiring and job composition rather than in a sudden rise in aggregate
unemployment. Early evidence already points in this direction: more AI-intensive sectors in the Eurozone have seen
weaker employment growth since late 2022, while younger and less-experienced white-collar workers in routine

cognitive occupations appear particularly exposed. AI may therefore initially show up through fewer entry-level
positions, slower hiring and weaker wage growth in exposed occupations rather than large-scale layoffs. The main
medium-term risk is a transition gap: firms can reorganize and automate tasks faster than workers can move into newly
created roles. Around 25mn jobs across the US and the major European economies are estimated to be subject to

automation risk, while new AI-related employment will only partly offset displacement in the near and medium term.
Over the longer term, we expect both substantial productivity gains and a moderately negative impact on
employment, but the gains are likely to be larger in the US than in Europe (Figure 12). Our ten-year estimates assume
that the AI transition follows a so-called J-curve, with investments and costs upfront, while the gains will only be realized

later⁴. AI-related investment already adds around 0.4pp to US GDP growth in 2026, but the immediate productivity gains
are limited as they get absorbed by reorganization costs and labor-market adjustment. By 2030, US GDP is projected
to grow  +0.6% faster annually, while employment will be 1.5% lower relative to a world without AI. Thereafter, the
productivity dividend becomes more visible: by 2036, cumulative labor productivity is around +4.8% above the no-AI

baseline in the US, while the employment effect largely unwinds to -0.1%, equivalent to roughly -170,000 jobs. In Europe,
productivity gains are more muted and employment effects more persistent: employment remains below the no-AI
baseline across the major economies, reaching around -2.9% in Spain (-650,000 jobs) and -3.1% in Italy (-740,000 jobs),
while Italy records the smallest productivity gain at around +1.8%. Germany and France also remain below baseline

on employment, although by less than Spain and Italy. However, these employment losses will unfold against a rapidly
shrinking workforce in the ageing European societies: by 2036, labor supply is set to decline by 5.6mn workers in
Germany, 4.2mn in Italy and 2.3mn in Spain. Europe therefore faces a double labor-market challenge from demographic
change and AI. The silver lining is that AI may partly compensate for a shrinking workforce in aggregate, but not

one-for-one, because AI-related displacement will not necessarily occur in the same occupations, sectors and regions
where labor shortages emerge. Overall, the US combines the highest near-term AI exposure with the smallest long-run
employment loss, reflecting faster adoption, stronger investment and more effective reallocation. Europe, by contrast,
faces a more persistent employment drag and smaller productivity gains, but part of the AI-related displacement could

help cushion the effects of ageing.

The preparedness gap is starkest in training:
adaptation is reaching those already ahead faster
than those most in need of it. Among employed

respondents, only 17% have received AI training at
work, while 44% say their employer offers none (Figure
13). Participation reaches 30% among employed
Americans but just 10% among Poles, and is markedly
higher for men than women, at 21% against 14%. The
education gradient is steeper still, with tertiary-educated
employees roughly three times as likely to have received
training as the secondary-educated. The generational
pattern is more nuanced: Millennials, rather than Gen

Z, record the highest training participation, promoter
share and productivity expectations, while Gen Z is more
likely to be offered training but decline it. The result is
a preparedness gap between anticipated disruption
and actual investment in adaptation. Left unchecked,
AI diffusion risks reinforcing existing advantages rather
than broadening opportunity.
Two demographic patterns sharpen that preparedness
challenge. The first is that the gender divide is often one

of uncertainty rather than outright opinion. Across multiple
questions, women are substantially more likely than men
to answer “don’t know”, with the gap reaching 25% versus
14% on AI’s effect on inequality and 28% versus 20% on
whether their own job will change. Women are therefore
not consistently more hostile to AI; they are less certain
about its implications. The second is a recurring U-shape
by financial strain. Both the financially comfortable and

the most strained tend to be more engaged and more
polarized than those in the middle, whether in their overall
attitudes, expected job disruption or willingness to retrain.
Even among respondents who find it very easy to make
ends meet, a majority still see AI as negative for jobs.
Labor-market anxiety is therefore not confined to the
economically vulnerable.
Preparedness: so far training
reaches mainly those already
ahead

### Page 21

14 October 2026
21
Beyond their own jobs, people expect AI to widen the
gap between winners and losers (Figure 14). Across
the sample, 37% expect AI to increase inequality in
their country, against just 9% who expect it to narrow,
for a net of +28%. This concern is shared across all
countries, from Austria (+40%) to Poland (+20%). The
fear is not simply that AI will replace jobs, but that its
benefits will accrue to some while others bear the costs.

Together with widespread concerns about labor-market
disruption, these findings point to a broader anxiety
about who will benefit from the AI revolution. The
challenge is therefore not only to unlock AI‘s productivity
potential but also to ensure that its gains are widely
shared.
For most people, AI‘s biggest risks are not hypothetical
future scenarios but immediate threats to information,
privacy and jobs. Misinformation and information
manipulation top the list of concerns, cited by 25% of

respondents, followed by data privacy and misuse of
personal information (21%) and job losses (17%) (Figure
15). The pattern is remarkably consistent across countries,
from Austria to the US. The loss of human control over
advanced AI systems, a prominent concern in the AI-safety
debate, ranks fourth at 16%, while the concentration of
power among large technology companies is cited by 9%,
with particularly limited concern in the US, home to the
leading AI developers. Only 12% of respondents say they

have no concerns about AI at all. The message is clear:
People are worried less about what AI might become than
about the harm it can already cause.
Inequality, misinformation
and privacy concerns: people’s
worries culminate in a call for
stricter regulation

Faced with these risks, the public is sending

policymakers an increasingly clear message: AI needs
stronger guardrails.  Across the sample, the net regulation
score – the share favoring strict or some regulation minus
those opposing regulation – has risen to 78%, up from
61% in 2025 (Figure 16). Support is strongest in the UK
(85%) and Spain (82%) but exceeds 70% in every surveyed
country, while only around 5% favor no regulation.
Crucially, demand for safeguards is not confined to AI
skeptics: Even respondents who expect AI to improve

their own productivity want stronger oversight. People
appear willing to embrace AI‘s benefits, but not to accept
its risks unchecked. The survey also predates the recent
wave of high-profile AI-related cybersecurity incidents,
which could further heighten public attention to safety
and accountability⁵.
⁵ See: Financial Times (2026), “Hugging Face attack is a wake-up call about the risks of AI” , 3 September 2026.

### Page 24

Allianz Research
24
Governments have taken significantly different
approaches on AI regulation, with the US and the
EU representing contrasting models (Table 1). The
US has no comprehensive federal AI law, relying on a
patchwork of state-level legislation and existing sectoral
rules, alongside federal efforts to limit divergent state
requirements. California has emerged as a leading
state regulator, introducing requirements addressing
transparency, safety and specific uses of AI. The UK

also relies largely on existing sectoral and cross-cutting
legislation, with additional measures under consideration
where gaps emerge. The EU, by contrast, has established
a comprehensive, risk-based framework through its AI
Act. In force since 2024, its obligations are being phased
in through 2028, with member states responsible for much
of the national enforcement framework. Italy has also
introduced supplementary national legislation, including

criminal provisions⁶. Outside the EU, public demand for
regulation thus runs well ahead of the rules in place.
The policy challenge is not simply to regulate
more, but to regulate what matters without stifling
innovation. Too little regulation can leave firms with
weak incentives to account for safety risks and other
harms, while allowing concentration in areas such as
hardware and data to entrench incumbents. Too much
or poorly designed regulation can raise the cost of

experimentation, slow AI adoption and investment and
weigh disproportionately on smaller firms, which are
less able to absorb compliance costs⁷. The objective for
policymakers should therefore be to address harmful
externalities and preserve trust and competition while
keeping barriers to experimentation and diffusion as
low as possible. As public demand for regulation rises,
getting this balance right will become increasingly
important.

⁶ Italy‘s Law 132/2025 made it the first EU member state to enact a standalone national AI law, introducing new criminal offences for deepfakes and

AI misuse. In June 2026, two implementing decrees further operationalized the framework, covering enforcement, sanctions, and liability.
⁷ See: Bruegel, June 2026, “ The right balance: how to fix European Union artificial intelligence regulation ” ; Brookings, September 2024, “ Balancing
market innovation incentives and regulation in AI: Challenges and opportunities ”

### Page 25

14 October 2026
25
For Europe, the central challenge is not simply to accelerate AI adoption, but to turn widespread experimentation
into productive, inclusive and trusted use Curiosity is not the main constraint: Europeans have embraced AI almost as
widely as Americans, but the technology has made fewer inroads into the workplace, and workers are less prepared to
use it effectively. The priority is therefore to remove the barriers that prevent AI from translating into productivity gains

and broader economic benefits. This requires action on five fronts:
1. Move AI from private experimentation into productive workplace use.  General-purpose technologies like
AI generate productivity gains only after firms make large complementary investments. These investments
show up as costs today, dragging down earnings and productivity in the short run, the so-called productivity
J-curve. Because the costs are immediate while the benefits are delayed, private investment may fall short,

in particular for SMEs, which face disproportionately high costs for the same implementation effort⁸. Europe
should reduce these costs that are holding back implementation, especially for SMEs, through accelerated
depreciation or temporary tax credits for identifiable complementary investments such as software integration,
data infrastructure, cybersecurity and process redesign. Public support should be tied to actual deployment
in business processes and, where possible, measurable productivity or efficiency gains rather than generic AI

subscriptions.
2. Close the training gap by starting with employers. This is primarily a provision problem: many workers
receive no offer of AI training, while most of those offered it take it up or plan to do so. Employer-led training
should therefore be supported through training tax credits, public co-financing and individual learning accounts,
with a focus on occupation-specific skills and groups currently least served including women, mid-career workers,

the secondary-educated and employees in smaller firms. Europe already has useful instruments: Germany’s
“Qualifizierungschancengesetz” subsidizes training costs and, in some cases, wage costs for employees affected
by technological change, while the EU  encourages member states to develop individual learning accounts that
give workers portable training entitlements. Where firms receive public support for major AI investments, this
could be linked to a workforce-training plan.

3. Shift labor-market policy from protecting jobs to supporting transitions.  Because AI is likely to change tasks
and occupations more extensively than it eliminates jobs outright, governments need to identify adjustment
pressures before they translate into unemployment. Combining occupation-level AI-exposure indicators
with vacancy, wage and skill-demand data can help identify vulnerable workers and emerging employment
opportunities. Workers in exposed occupations should have access to retraining while still employed, stronger

job-matching services, mobility grants and temporary wage insurance where moving into a new role initially
means lower pay. Such measures must go beyond traditional active labor-market programs, whose short-term
effects are often limited and vary considerably across program types and participants. Better targeting is crucial:
Policy priorities:
adopting AI better, not just faster
⁸  For productivity discussion, see: Brynjolfsson, Rock & Syverson 2021. „The Productivity J-Curve: How Intangibles Complement General Purpose

Technologies,“ American Economic Journal: Macroeconomics. For SMEs discussion, see: OECD 2025, “ AI adoption by small and medium-sized enter -
prises”,

### Page 26

Allianz Research
26
programs that invest in human capital accumulation, such as classroom-based vocational training and on-the-
job traineeships, show large gains, in particular for the long-term unemployed, whose skills have eroded during
extended periods out of work, and for women⁹.  Recent evidence confirms that well-designed interventions
do indeed deliver: cost-benefit analyses show that such training schemes can be highly cost effective, and

programs combining part-time work placements with training and short-term community work placements for
the unemployed produce lasting earnings gains for participants¹⁰. The key lesson is therefore to replace broad,
expensive programs with measures that are tightly linked to real-time labor-market signals.
4. Make sure the productivity dividend is broadly shared. AI is likely to raise productivity but could also
shift national income from labor to capital, and if automation outruns capital accumulation, wages could fall

even while output rises¹¹. Policy should therefore address both labor income and household wealth. On the
income side, broader access to skills can help translate productivity gains into better employment prospects
and wages. . On the asset side, funded pensions, employee share plans and wider household capital-market
participation can help more people benefit directly from AI-driven returns, particularly in Europe, where  equity
ownership is heavily concentrated among wealthier households¹². At the same time, the tax system should

avoid unnecessarily favoring capital over labor and adapt if a larger share of income shifts toward capital and
economic rents. Europe is particularly exposed here, as its tax systems rely more heavily on labor income taxes
and social contributions than the US, potentially putting greater pressure on public finances if the labor share of
income declines¹³.
5. Strengthen trust by targeting the risks people actually identify. Misinformation, data misuse and labor-

and human oversight and appeal rights for high-impact AI decisions. Rules should be credible and proportionate:
safeguards that build trust can support adoption, while complex or inconsistent requirements can slow diffusion,
especially for smaller firms. EU-wide templates, sector-specific guidance and regulatory sandboxes can help
make implementation more predictable without weakening protections.
⁹ See: Card D., Kluve J., Weber A., 2018. “What works? A Meta Analysis of Recent Active Labor Market Program Evaluations”, Journal of the European

Economic Association.
¹⁰ See evidence on Slovenia active labor market policies: OECD, 2026. “ The wide-ranging effects of active labour market policies in Slovenia: Lessons
learnt and implications for other countries ” ; and evidence on the effectiveness of Ireland’s Community Employment and Tús schemes: OECD, 2024:
“Impact Evaluation of Ireland’s Active Labour Market Policies ”,
¹¹  Korinek & Suh, 2024. Scenarios for the transition to AGI, NBER WP 32255.

¹² See: ECB, 15 September 2026. “ Why Europeans save differently, and how to put those savings to work ”
¹³  The Economist, 29 September 2026. “ How the AI boom could worsen the rich world’s fiscal crunch ”

The survey queried more than 8,000 respondents across eight countries: Austria, France, Germany, Italy, Poland, Spain,
the United Kingdom and the United States. It was conducted as a web-based survey between 22 April and 25 May 2026.
Overall responsibility lies with Allianz Research, Allianz Investment Management SE. Sample planning and fieldwork

were carried out by Qualtrics.
Respondents were selected using representative quota sampling. Qualtrics was given quotas specifying how many
people to survey in each country and which selection criteria to apply, with quotas set in line with official statistics for
gender and age groups. A comparison with official statistics confirms that the resulting sample broadly corresponds to
the population aged 18 and over in each of the eight countries.

## Document 270: Cliff Notes_ getting the balance right (2026-10-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ getting the balance right (2026-10-20).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
10:46 October 16 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September Labour Force Survey. Employment surprised
to the downside, -39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force participation from August’s near-
historic high largely offset. The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August was revised up.

Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless, the take home point from the update is that the

### Page 2

labour market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs below working-age population growth.
We expect the unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter financial conditions and cost-of-
living pressures restrict activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast peak is near at
4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the communication challenge for the RBA. The
September minutes were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing on materialising

upside risks to inflation. But, with the unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption of
4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the labour market is becoming harder to defend.
Still, with a clear focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our near-term CPI forecasts in
the October Market Outlook – we still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in September, taking the annual rate up to

3.7%yr. Energy prices were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation was a touch above
consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr). Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months (0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4%
(3.1%yr) on a 0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport services and a 0.3%
increase in medical care services (2.7%yr and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release of

the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures, with the headline and core measures up
6.3%yr and 5.0%yr respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these developments, firms
across multiple districts citing tariffs, insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost
pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect further price increases as contracts/hedges
roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is enough signal in these reports to warrant a

follow-up hike at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee members believe they are
near the end of the tightening cycle and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman both
showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear bias towards an October or December decision.
Williams stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in this cycle given known information.
And Waller also showed confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are doing some of the

FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating
that one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation near the 2.0%yr medium-term target.
However, as discussed in this month’s Market Outlook, capacity pressures are likely to see inflation
consistently above target, rather than below, in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity
in the housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to remain modestly restrictive over

the forecast horizon and will also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade account. At USD133.5bn, the September
result was a whisker inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More impressively, throughout
2025 and 2026 year-to-date the surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the average of
2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth of export growth is as impressive as the headline

annual gain of 36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese investment in
neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time, both trade and income receipts from offshore
investment should buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound foreign investment should
moderate the pace of gains against other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.

### Page 3

Related articles
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the evolution of producer prices across
Asia, and the likelihood of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent decades
continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was one of the key drivers of declining goods prices.
While still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing development to instead be driven by
capital, technology and energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking production are

also increasingly prioritising resilience, security and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore
more probably future supply-chain developments will help contain rising costs through productivity than
drive sustained price declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.

## Document 273: Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds

Source PDF: all report pdf/Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Compelling risk-reward in 
US 10-year bonds  
Summary 
Event: The US 10 -year government bond yield has surged 
past 5.0% to a 19-year high and has now entered our three -
month forecast range of 5.0-5.25%. At the time of writing, the 
10-year yield is trading at 5.2%, its highest since June 2007’s 
high of 5.32%. The next resistance levels are the 2002 high 
of 5.47% and the 2001 high of 5.53%.  
We view the risk/reward for the US 10-year government 
bond after the recent yield spike as  compelling and we 
are initiating a long opportunistic idea at the current 
yield. As the table on the right shows, if the 10 -year yield 
rises to 6%, investors would face less than 1% in negative 
total returns over a one-year period, whereas if the yield falls 
below 5.0%, they would gain at least 6% in total returns.  
While we continue to prefer the 3-7-year bond maturity range 
for income generation (the US 5-year bond yield is also 
above 5%), and broadly prefer corporate over government 
bonds amid solid corporate fundamentals, the US 10 -year 
government bond yield has finally reached reasonable levels 
for investors looking for slightly longer maturities. 
Rationale: We expect the US 10-year government bond yield 
to ease to 4.75-5.0% over a 12-month period due to: 
Easing oil prices and inflation over next 12 months: While 
the yield is likely to remain elevated in the next three months 
due to elevated oil prices caused by geopolitical uncertainty, 
heavy AI investment-driven bond issuance and a resilient US 
economy, it is likely to ease towards the 4.75 -5.0% range 
over 12  months as oil prices moderate either due to a 
resolution in the Middle East or a rerouting of Middle East 
energy supplies and as the impact of tariffs fade.  
We expect s hallower Fed rate hikes than market s: We 
expect a total of 50bps of Fed rate hikes by June 2027, before 
a cut in H2 as US inflation eventually eases towards the Fed’s 
2% target. This  rate trajectory is shallower than c. 95bps of 
further hikes priced by markets over the next 12 months. 
 The benchmark US 10-year government bond yield has 
risen to its highest since 2007, offering medium-term 
investors a compelling risk-reward trade-off

### Page 2

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Investor positioning adjustment: In the very near-term, the 
US 10 -year bond yield has room to move lower as hedge 
funds using systematic strategies (CTAs) are extremely short 
US government bonds, raising the chance of short -covering 
if upcoming jobs and inflation data miss estimates. 
A shallow Fed hiking cycle  
We expect the shallowest Fed hiking cycle in almost 
three decades. This should enable the US 10-year yield to 
ease towards 4.75 -5.0% over a 12 -month period. Following 
this month’s increase, we forecast two further 25bp hikes by 
June 2027, followed by a cut in H2 2027. Markets price at 
least three more hikes by June and a strong probability of a 
fourth by September. Our projected 75bp s of total hikes this 
cycle compares with 525bps of hikes in 2022–23, 225bps in 
2015–18, 425bps in 2004–06 and 175bp in 1999–2000. The 
last smaller mid-cycle move was a single 25bps hike in 1997. 
Why we expect a more cautious Fed 
The latest hike restored the Fed’s credibility after renewed 
oil-price pressures, elevated inflation and a balanced labour 
market prompted a unanimous policy vote. However, the 
following factors argue against aggressive tightening: 
Supply-driven inflation: Energy costs and AI-infrastructure 
constraints, rather than excess demand, are driving prices. 
Higher rates cannot repair supply and may weaken growth. 
Labour is no longer the main inflation source:  Labour 
supply and demand are better balanced, wage growth has 
moderated and unemployment is near full employment. AI 
further weakens the traditional Phillips Curve relationship 
between unemployment and inflation. 
Disinflation should resume:  Energy pressures may fade 
after the US mid-term elections, while the one-off inflationary 
effects of tariffs should diminish after 2025–26. 
AI raises the neutral rate , but dampens inflation:  
Investment and productivity may lift the neutral rate ( r*), the 
rate consistent with stable inflation and full employment, 
while efficiency gains limit wage-price spirals. 
Markets are already tightening  financial conditions : 
Higher bond yields have raised borrowing costs and 
restrained activity, doing part of the Fed’s work. 
The cycle began from a higher base:  The Fed funds rate 
started at 3.75%, versus 0.25% in the previous two cycles 
and 1% before that, reducing additional tightening required. 
Monetary transmission has weakened:  Wealthier 
households drive more spending but are less rate -sensitive, 
while lower -income consumers are already constrained. 
Further hikes may , therefore, curb demand less effectively 
while causing disproportionate harm to low-income families. 
 We expect a shallower Fed rate hiking cycle compared 
with market expectations

### Page 3

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Small business conditions are deteriorating:  Higher 
financing and energy costs are weakening sentiment, as 
reflected in the latest Beige Book . The renewed 
underperformance of US small capitalisation (S&P 600) and 
mid capitalisation (S&P 400) equity indices  versus the large 
cap S&P 500 index over the past couple of months also show 
rising pressure on small businesses. 
Fiscal and financial -stability risks are rising:  Aggressive 
Fed tightening would increase US debt-servicing costs as the 
US Treasury seeks cheaper funding through shorter-maturity 
issuance, while potentially amplifying financial-system stress. 
Upside risk to bond yields 
Our view of lower bond yields carries the following risks: 
Persistent inflation:  Repeated supply shocks, strong AI -
related investment demand and tighter immigration controls 
could keep inflation above target and force steeper Fed rate 
hikes. With unemployment near the Fed’s estimate of full 
employment, a sharp drop in labour supply could intensify 
wage pressures and destabilise inflation expectations . We 
note that long-term expectations remain contained for now. 
Geopolitical escalation:  An intensification of geopolitical 
conflicts after the mid -term elections could lift energy prices 
and inflation. However, a Democrat -controlled or divided 
Congress may constrain US foreign-policy initiatives through 
tighter budgets, while Republicans could turn less supportive 
as attention shifts to the 2028 presidential race. 
Higher neutral rate: AI investments could raise the neutral 
rate (r*), turning current monetary policy settings 
accommodative. This could necessitate further rate hikes. 
However, the Fed has barely raised its nominal neutral rate 
estimate, lifting it to 3.2% in September from 3.0% a year ago. 
Bottomline: The outlook for rates remains geopolitics 
dependent, with all Fed meetings live . However, based on 
our core scenario, we see the US 10-year yield settling lower 
in the 4.75-5.0% in 6-12 months. A shallow Fed hiking cycle 
due to expected disinflation offers investors a reasonably 
attractive risk-reward in US 10-year government bonds. 
US 30-year government bonds , on the other hand, may 
continue to see elevated yields as the US fiscal deficit is 
unlikely to have improved next year, and may look much 
worse. As a result, “term premium” may keep the ultra -long 
end elevated. Short of some unorthodox policy to artifici ally 
cap them lower, the 30-year yield is likely to continue to drift 
higher towards the 5.5-5.75% range into H2 2027. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist 
Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

tariff effects on inflation to fade over 12 months, 
offsetting AI-driven effects on bond yields

## Document 293: EcoNews of 19 October 2026 (2026-10-19)

Source PDF: all report pdf/EcoNews of 19 October 2026 (2026-10-19).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
5
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
IMF ANNUAL MEETINGS
Resilience, but no relief. The IMF's latest global growth forecast is un-
changed from a year ago and from April 2026 (at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4%
in 2027), despite the energy shock, however with a different geogra -
phical breakdown: many energy importers and war-exposed countries
downgraded, and AI-exposed countries upgraded. Resilience was the

buzzword of the meetings, however it appeared mostly in the rear -
view mirror. Looking ahead, buffers are running thin, fiscal policy needs
urgent consolidation, and downside risks (from energy and AI exube -
rance) loom large. Global headline inflation is expected to rise to 4.7%
in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before easing back to 4.1% in
2027 (revised +0.2pp). Growth in advanced economies is revised up to
2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany), staying at 1.8% in 2027. Eu-

rozone growth is upgraded to 1.1% in 2026 (+0.2pp) and 1.2% in 2027.
US growth stays projected at 2.3% in 2026 and 2027 (+0.1pp). Germany
sees the largest G7 upgrade, to 1.3% in 2026 (+0.6pp) and 1.1% in 2027
(+0.1pp). France is the only major advanced economy downgraded, to
0.4% in 2026 (-0.2pp) and 0.7% in 2027 (-0.2pp). For more detailed
takeaways see Substack and this week’s EcoWeek editorial.
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Trade policy, competitiveness and budget agreements by year-end.

EU leaders asked the Commission to propose by year-end an instru -
ment allowing, in extreme cases, full closure of the single market to
a rival deemed to be attacking the EU economy. It is designed as a
“second-strike” deterrent against unfair practices and retaliation.
The Commission is also exploring extending the hybrid-vehicle safe -
guards approach agreed with China last week to other sectors such
as chemicals and machinery. On the "Made in Europe" label, vehicles

would need to be assembled in Europe with at least 70% of compo -
nent value (excluding batteries) sourced locally and with substantial
R&D in the EU. The UK would be included, provided London aligns with
EU trade defence measures. EU Council also called for decisive pro -
gress on the competitiveness agenda, with the single-market (inclu -
ding 26th regime) and simplification files to be wrapped up by year-end.
On the multi-annual financial framework, the objective remains a deal

by end-2026, so that legislation can be adopted in 2027.
EURO AREA
Moderate tightening does it. Multiple ECB Governing Council members,
speaking from the IMF Meetings in Bangkok, re-underwrote their
“moderate response” approach to the ongoing energy-driven infla -
tion surge. Accordingly, they signaled that additional rate action was
likely but not imminent, acknowledging that the rise in bond yields
was doing part of the job, and that there were no signs yet of second

round effects. Strengthening the case for moderation, the Eurozone in-
dustrial production fell in August for the second month running, by
0.5% m/m, pulled down by pharmaceutical output and motor vehicles.
That said, production in Netherlands surged to a new high (2.5% m/m
and 8.7% y/y), driven by AI machinery and equipment.
France
Business starts booming, insolvencies are stable, growth outlook is
downgraded. Business creations rose by 16% y/y in the 3 rd quarter

(+23% y/y in September), driven mainly by the digital sector and retail.
In parallel, business bankruptcies stabilised in the 3rd quarter according
to Altares, with a 0.5% increase y/y (compared with a 5.4% increase y/y
in Q2), and their impact on employment declined: 50,510 jobs at risk
in Q3, down 3% y/y (see our recent analysis). The Banque de France
halved its estimate for Q3 GDP to 0.1% q/q (same as Insee) due to
the manufacturing sector. Its monthly survey showed a sharp jump in

business uncertainty in September, driven by fuel prices and economic
deterioration (the most acute readings in industry and services since
April). The OAT-bund spread was rangebound (137-142).
Germany
Producer prices are rising. Wholesale prices rose 8.7% y/y (vs. 6.8% in
August) due to higher wholesale prices for energy products and inter -
mediate goods, particularly metals.
UNITED KINGDOM
Summer outperformance and kudos from IMF. Monthly GDP defied

expectations in August and posted a 0.1% m/m against a -0.1% consen-
sus. This marked a slowdown from July (0.4% m/m), but also a fourth
consecutive month of expansion. Industrial production underpinned
the advance: it accelerated to 0.8% m/m (consensus: -0.1%, prior: 0.3%)
thanks to pharmaceuticals. The IMF sees growth accelerating to 1.3%
in 2026 (+0.3 pp since July) and 1.2% in 2027. It also singled out the UK
among advanced economies for its efforts and progress in putting its

fiscal house in order.
UNITED STATES
Inflation heats up, manufacturing accelerates, but an October hike
is unlikely. CPI inflation exceeded expectations at 0.65% m/m in Sep -
tember (up 0.2pp), the fastest monthly pace since April. Accordingly,
the y/y rate climbed to 3.7% (up 0.3pp). The biggest upside surprise,
however, came from the core CPI: at 0.33% m/m (consensus: 0.2%, prior:
0.3%) and 2.6% y/y (consensus: 2.5%, prior: 2.4%); as services remained

sticky while goods accelerated to 0.3% m/m on autos and AI-related
items. Producer prices too rose well above expectations to 1.3% m/m
(consensus: 0.6%, prior: 0.5%) – the largest monthly increase since
March 2022 – and 6.3% y/y (consensus: 5.3%, prior: 5.6%). Industrial
production accelerated in September to 0.7% m/m (up 0.6pp) thanks
to utilities. Manufacturing returned to growth (0.3% m/m, up 0.5pp)
with gains broadening beyond the AI-related and aerospace sectors.

Retail sales excl. autos and gasoline, by contrast, slowed sharply to
0.2% m/m (down 0.9pp). During his speech at the IMF Meetings after
the hotter than expected inflation prints, Chair Warsh declined to hint
at an imminent rate hike. We continue to expect the next rate hike to
occur in December.
To watch this week:
- Developed markets Flash PMIs for October (Friday).
- Eurozone Germany September PPI, October ZEW (Tuesday), France Insee Business Climate (Thursday).

- UK August Employment data (Tuesday), September CPI and PPI (Wednesday), September Retail Sales (Friday).
- Japan September CPI, October PMI (Friday).

### Page 2

6
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
JAPAN
Producer price inflation eases. Producer prices fell for a second month
in a row in September, down a further 0.5% m/m, while the yearly rate
slowed for the first time since February (-0.8pp), though it remains
firm at 6.8%. The IMF forecasts growth at 0.7% in both 2026 and 2027,
broadly unchanged. The yen weakened past 158 per dollar again after

reports that the BoJ is leaning against a back-to-back October rate hike.
EMERGING ECONOMIES
Emerging markets
Asia’s outlook upgraded, other regions downgraded.
The IMF upgraded its Asia growth forecast to 4.8% in 2026 and 4.4% in
2027. The largest upgrades are for economies well integrated into the
AI supply chain, notably Taiwan (+5.5pp to 10.7%), South Korea (+0.7pp
to 3.3%), Singapore, Vietnam, Malaysia and to some extent Thailand.
India is forecast to grow 7% in both 2026 and 2027 (fiscal year basis),

up 0.6 and 0.3pp from July on robust domestic demand.
Central Europe’s GDP growth is projected at 2.1% in 2026, slightly
lower than April’s forecast. In 2027, it remains unchanged at 2.3%.
Growth paths are diverse within the region.
In the Middle East & North Africa, GDP is expected to contract by 2%
in 2026. GCC real GDP should fall by 4.8% with only Oman expected
to post positive growth (4.1%). A marked rebound is expected for 2027
(MENA: 7%; GCC: 9.1%).

The growth outlook for Latin America remains subdued, revised down
to 2.3% in 2026 (from 2.4%) and 2.5% in 2027 (from 2.7%). However, in
its "International Trade Outlook 2026" published on 15 October, the
Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) ex-
pects the region to experience a trade boom in 2026: goods exports are
projected to surge by 20% in value terms (11% from price increases and
9% from volume growth), with trade to Asia (excluding China) soaring

by 32%, while intraregional trade growth lags behind at 8%. Yet, these
export gains are concentrated in commodities and low-value-added
manufacturing, limiting their spillover to broader growth. Stronger
trade is thus providing only a modest boost to the economy.
China
Explosive export growth, subdued inflation and credit growth. In Sep-
tember, export value growth surged to 36.3% y/y from 25% in August,
driven by semiconductors, computer equipment and other AI goods, as

well as green technologies. Export prices surged, especially for semi -
conductors, while total export volumes remained robust. Import value
growth also accelerated to 31% y/y from 28.2% in August, boosted by
both rising volume and rapidly increasing prices. The trade surplus
hit a new monthly record of USD 134 bn in September. CPI inflation
edged up to 0.9% y/y in September, mainly because of a 14.9% rise in
vehicle fuel prices. Food prices continued to fall (-1.7% y/y) and core

inflation was stable at 1%. Producer price inflation accelerated to 5%
(the highest since 2022) due to rising commodity prices. Growth in to-
tal domestic credit outstanding (“social financing”) continued to slow
down, reaching a record low of 7.1% y/y in September. Bank loans to
households declined (-2% y/y) for the seventh consecutive month.
In Q3 2026, real GDP growth rebounded to 4.5% y/y (1.2% q/q) from
4.3% y/y (0.9% q/q), primarily led by industrial production (which rose by

5.1% y/y on average in Q3 vs. 4.6% in Q2). On the demand side, exports
were the key driver while fixed-asset investment continued to contract.
Retail sales posted a small pickup in September but remained flat
in real terms in Q3 as a whole. With growth reaching 4.6% y/y year-to-
date, China needs only a 4.2% growth in Q4 to meet the official target of
4.5%-5% for this year – which seems easily feasible. The IMF trimmed
its growth forecast by 0.1pp to 4.5% in 2026 and 4% in 2027.

Indonesia
MSCI launched a consultation on reclassifying the country from Emer-
ging Market to Standalone Market status, with a final decision due by
end-June 2027, citing opaque shareholding structures and suspected
coordinating trading. Since January 2026, MSCI had frozen Index ad -
justments and threatened only a downgrade to Frontier market status
(like Pakistan), but market participants argued that a Frontier reclas -
sification would not properly reflect the extent of the market’s pro -

blems. Over the medium term, the risk is heavy foreign capital outflows
at a time when net FDI no longer covers the current account deficit.
This leaves Indonesia reliant on foreign portfolio inflows and the ru -
piah exposed to a reversal in sentiment. On the day of this announce -
ment, the Jakarta Composite Index fell by 11.6%, its steepest drop since
January 1998. Among Asian emerging markets, Indonesia has posted
the worst stock market performance since the beginning of the year

(-40.7%).
COMMODITIES
Iran/Oil. More of the same. Brent oil prices have remained broadly
stable this week (0.5% at 107$/b on Monday). Renewed Iranian at -
tacks on tankers in the Strait and intensification of Houthis’ attack over
the Saudi territory feed the geopolitical risk premium on barrel prices.
But IEA announcement of an accelerated 100 mb oil release (above
the March initial target of 400 mb) and reports that the US may lift
sanctions on the Russian and Iranian shadow fleets have contributed to

crude price stability. The spread with physical barrel (Dated Brent) has
declined (6.7$/b on Oct 16th). Trump administration’s various attempts
to lower diesel prices have had a limited impact for now. European gas
prices (TTF) recorded another weekly rise (4.7% w/w to 88.4 €/MWh,
the highest level since Dec 22nd, 2022) on the back of the rising number
of attacks on vessels in the Gulf and persisting disruption in the Strait.
However, EU gas inventories reached 74% of full capacities on October

16th (i.e., almost at the 80% month-end target), which should reduce
support for TTF prices going forward.
y/y = year-on-year
m/m = month-on-month
bp = basis point
pp = percentage point
mb = million barrels

## Document 309: FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus FEMO's Steady Climb

Source PDF: all report pdf/FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus  FEMO's Steady Climb.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus  FEMO's Steady Climb
Date:    5 hours ago
FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus FEMO's Steady Climb

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
FOMO's Rollercoaster Versus
FEMO's Steady Climb
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 19 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Jonny Gios / Unsplash
I. FOMO vs FEMO
Forecasting the stock market is easy! Just multiply the P/E (price-to-
earnings ratio) by E (earnings per share) to get P (the stock price index).
The hard part, of course, is getting the P/E and E right. We call the P/E
component FOMO (fear of missing out) and the E component FEMO
(fabulous earnings momentum). This year, investors have been on a
rollercoaster ride as FOMO has fluctuated mostly to the downside, while

FEMO has continued climbing.

### Page 3

More recently, concerns about AI investment returns and rising US
Treasury yields have tightened credit conditions, raising fears about the
sustainability of the AI investment boom and weighing on P/Es. Since
early October, multiple compression has outpaced continued earnings
gains, dragging many stocks lower.
Semiconductors have been at the center of the recent rout, with the
semiconductor stock price index plunging 15.7% over eight consecutive
sessions. However, FOMO staged a comeback today, with the Nasdaq

rising 1.1% and semiconductors gaining 1.6%, snapping their losing
streak. The FOMO rollercoaster continues, but FEMO remains firmly on
an upward trajectory.
Here is more:
(1) S&P 500. So far this year, forward earnings surged 31.0% ytd through
October 19, more than offsetting a 14.2% contraction in the forward P/E.
Consequently, the index is up 12.3% ytd (chart).
(2) Information Technology. The divergence is even more striking in the
S&P 500 Information Technology sector. Forward earnings has soared

65.5% ytd, while the forward P/E has contracted 25.6%, leaving the
sector's stock price index up 23.0% ytd (chart). Since early October,
however, falling P/Es have outpaced rising earnings, pulling IT stock
prices lower.

### Page 4

(3) Semiconductors. FEMO is incredibly strong in the S&P 500
Semiconductors industry, which now accounts for a record 55.0% of the IT
sector's forward earnings and 40.9% of its market capitalization (chart).
That implies a significant forward P/E discount to the rest of the IT sector
and to the S&P 500. The discount reflects concerns that rapidly rising
borrowing costs for highly leveraged AI infrastructure providers could slow
the AI investment boom and weaken demand for chips. Semiconductor

earnings continue to soar, but that's because capacity constraints are
boosting semiconductor prices, which should fall sooner or later as either
demand slows or capacity expands, or both.
(4) Healthy constraints. We view investors' growing concerns about the
AI boom as good news. Financial, social, political, and physical
constraints should reduce overinvestment risks and keep the boom on a
sustainable path. Recently, financial constraints have been in focus, with

### Page 5

CoreWeave's bond yields surging above 15% last week as the Bond
Vigilantes increasingly scrutinized AI borrowers. The bond market is
playing a welcome role in allocating capital more efficiently. Meanwhile,
the big hyperscalers remain the boom's main drivers, supported by strong
cash flows and ample access to capital. The AI rollercoaster may be
hitting speed bumps, but that will help keep it on a steadier path.
II. Industrial Production
The strength of the US economy continues to underpin strong earnings

growth. Industrial production rose 0.7% m/m in September, its biggest
gain since April, and 2.2% y/y, the strongest since September 2022
(charts). Leading the way higher was utilities output. That reflects rising
electricity demand from data centers and manufacturing, as well as
weather-related demand. Manufacturing output edged up 0.3%.

### Page 6

Since ChatGPT was released in late 2022, technology-related industrial
production has risen sharply to record highs (chart).
Defense and space equipment production also continues to increase at a
solid pace, supported by rising military spending and the rapid growth of
the space industry (chart).

### Page 7

III. Federal Deficit
Fiscal policy also continues to boost economic growth, though at the cost
of large federal deficits and mounting federal debt. The federal budget
deficit totaled $1.99 trillion over the 12 months through September, while
marketable Treasury debt increased by $2.12 trillion (chart).
Federal outlays reached $7.40 trillion, including $3.77 trillion for health
care, Medicare, and Social Security (chart). Notably, net interest payments

reached a record $1.05 trillion, exceeding defense spending of $970
billion.

### Page 8

Tax receipts totaled $5.40 trillion, supported by rising individual income
and payroll taxes as well as tariff revenues (chart). Corporate income tax
receipts have been depressed this year by the 100% depreciation
allowance granted by last year's One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBB).
IV. Foreign Capital Flows
Given Corporate America's FEMO, it is not surprising that foreign capital
continues to pour into US financial markets. Over the 12 months through

August, net foreign purchases of US equities totaled a whopping $1.01
trillion, followed by corporate bonds and Treasury notes and bonds (chart).
Apparently, foreign investors have a greater appetite for US equities and
corporate bonds than for US Treasuries.

### Page 9

Net foreign equity inflows surged to a record high, far exceeding previous
peaks (chart). Private investors accounted for $844 billion, while foreign
official institutions contributed another $165 billion, a remarkable
development given their historically limited participation in US equity
markets.
Foreigners purchased more US corporate bonds than US Treasury notes
and bonds over the past 12 months (chart).

### Page 10

clause.

## Document 336: Leading Index still tracking a touch below trend (2026-10-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Leading Index still tracking a touch below trend (2026-10-20).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
WESTPAC-MI LEADING
INDEX BULLETIN
14 October 2026
Key points
• Index growth rate holds at –0.03% in
September, unchanged from August.
• Momentum is a touch below trend
heading into late 2026/early 2027.
• Mixed detail suggests the economy
lacking a clear direction.

Westpac-MI Leading Index
Latest insights into economic momentum

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The six-month annualised growth rate in the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Leading Index, which indicates the
likely pace of economic activity relative to trend three to
nine months into the future, held at –0.03% in September,
unchanged from August.

The Leading Index continues to show moderately improved
momentum compared to the middle of the year, with the
latest reads pointing to growth holding at or slightly below
trend heading into late 2026/early 2027. Growth appears
to be stabilising at a slow pace rather than stalling. That
said, both the latest fuel prices and the RBA’s September
interest rate increase are still working their way through to
the economy with impacts likely to take some of the gloss off

growth near term. Overall, Westpac expects annual growth
to come in at 1.5% for calendar 2026, a below-trend result.
The Leading Index growth rate has firmed slightly over the
last six months, rising from –0.16% back in March to –0.03%
in September. The component detail shows mixed moves over
the period. Four components have contributed more positively
with reduced drags from the Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Unemployment Expectations Index (adding +0.23ppts to

the Index growth rate); the Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Consumer Expectations Index (adding another +0.15ppts);
and the S&P/ASX200 (+0.15ppts) combining with a modest
lift in growth in US industrial production (adding +0.12ppts).
These positives have been partially offset by modest drags
from four previously stable components: dwelling approvals
(–0.34ppts); monthly hours worked (–0.08ppts); commodity
prices, measured in AUD terms (–0.07ppts) and the yield

spread (–0.05ppts).
Overall, the component mix speaks to a lack of direction.
Indeed, despite some large shocks over the last year, the
Leading Index growth rate has been relatively contained since
early 2025, holding in a narrow –0.3% to +0.5% range. This
compares to history which, outside of the COVID and GFC
shocks, has seen growth rates move between a much wider
–1.9% to +1.6% range. Of course, the apparent stability,
which is evident across all components of the index, may not

last but for now it suggests the economy has been stuck in a
bit of a holding pattern over the last two years.
Leading Index still tracking a touch below trend
The Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index of
Economic Activity is designed to assist in identifying
turning points in the economy. The index combines
variables that reflect different aspects of the economy
into a single index that generally produces a more reliable
cyclical indicator than any single component taken

individually. The Leading Index of Economic Activity
provides advance information on the state of the economy
and gives early warnings of cyclical turning points.
Matthew Hassan
Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting
**Please note that this will be the last Westpac press release for
the monthly Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index. We will
continue to publish regular bulletin updates through our westpaciq.
com.au website.

ANALYST: Tajinder Dhillon, CFA October 1, 2026 
tajinder.dhillon@lseg.com; 
THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q3 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 30.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 26.8%. 
 Of the 13 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q3, 84.6% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.7% and prior four quarter average of 81.5%. 
 26Q3 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 11.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 11.2%. 
 76.9% of companies have reported 26Q3 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 63% and an average over the past four quarters of 76.8%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 40 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 65 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 40 by 65 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q4– 27Q3) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.1. 
 During the week of Oct. 5, three S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
This Week in Earnings provides analysis and commentary on aggregate earnings estimate revisions, growth rates and valuations.  2 
There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $773.2B 
from $760.7B). Seven of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since Aug. 1, the materials (-9.1%) and health care (-2.2%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases 
in earnings, while the energy (16.3%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in earnings. Overall, share-
weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 1.6% since the start of the quarter.

Since Aug. 1, the health care (-1.7B) and materials (-1.5B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the information technology (8.3B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 12.5B since the start of the quarter.

13 companies in the S&P 500 Index have reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these companies, 84.6% reported 
earnings above analyst expectations and 7.7% reported earnings below analyst expectations. In a typical quarter 
(since 1994), 67% of companies beat estimates and 20% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 82% of 
companies beat the estimates and 15% missed estimates. 
In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 5.3% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
1994) average surprise factor of 4.4% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 7.7%.

In aggregate, companies are reporting revenues that are 2.4% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
2002) average surprise factor of 1.3% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 2.5%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 30.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 26.8%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $773.2B in 26Q3, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $591.9B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q3. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q3. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Consumer Staples 
sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (114.7%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $55.1B in 26Q3, 
compared to earnings of $25.7B in 25Q3. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (445.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (116.1%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 25.1%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (66.5%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $256.1B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $153.8B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (135.6%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.9%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 25.3%. 
The Consumer Staples sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $33.7B 
in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $32.6B in 25Q3. 3 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Packaged Foods & Meats (-6.5%) and Household Products (-4.2%) sub-industries have 
the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 6.4%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q4 is 28.5%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 25.5%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $804.2B in 26Q4, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $625.7B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q4. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q4. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Real Estate sector 
has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q4. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (102.8%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $49.2B in 26Q4, 
compared to earnings of $24.3B in 25Q4. 3 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (230.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (129.3%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 39.4%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (54.9%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $289.1B in 26Q4, compared to earnings of $186.6B in 25Q4. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (117%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.8%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 18.4%. 
The Real Estate sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (4.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $15.2B in 
26Q4, compared to earnings of $14.6B in 25Q4. 2 of the 13 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Telecom Tower Reits (-13.3%) and Multi-Family Residential Reits (-0.1%) sub-industries 
have the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 7.1%.

Real Estate 47.0 42.7 4.4 10.3% 
Information Technology 764.6 547.9 216.6 39.5% 
Communication Services 359.4 318.6 40.8 12.8% 
Utilities 135.2 123.7 11.5 9.3% 
S&P 500 4,826.7 4,324.7 502.0 11.6%

Source: LSEG I/B/E/S 
The estimated revenue growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 11.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth rate declines  to 11.2%. The S&P 
500 expects to see revenue of $4826.7B in 26Q3, compared to revenue of $4324.7B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 
constituents) in 25Q3.

11 of the 11 sectors anticipate revenue growth for the quarter. The Information Technology sector has the highest revenue growth rate for the 
quarter, while the Health Care sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3.

The Information Technology sector has the highest revenue growth rate (39.5%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $764.6B in 26Q3, compared to 
revenue of $547.9B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher revenue than a year ago. The Semiconductors 
(87.7%) and Semiconductor Materials & Equipment (48.2%) sub-industries have the highest revenue growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are 
removed, the growth rate declines to 22.1%.

The Health Care sector has the lowest revenue growth rate (4%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $958.4B in 26Q3, compared to revenue of 
$921.2B in 25Q3. 1 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower revenue than a year ago. The Health Care Supplies (-3.1%) 
and Managed Health Care (1.2%) sub-industries have the lowest revenue growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed , the growth rate 
improves to 5.1%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted

average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate),
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time.
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.

StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.
Over the next two weeks, 31 S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, three positive

surprises and five negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

## Document 403: US MARKET CALL_ Yes, AI Is Too Big To Fail

Source PDF: all report pdf/US MARKET CALL_ Yes, AI Is Too Big To Fail.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-17
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US MARKET CALL: Yes, AI Is Too Big To Fail
Date:    3 hours ago
US MARKET CALL: Yes, AI Is Too Big To Fail

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US MARKET CALL: Yes, AI Is
Too Big To Fail
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 17 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Nils Rasmusson / Unsplash
I. Bonds
We've all been suffering from AI fatigue for a while. The daily news about it
has been head-spinning. We've been warned that AI is a bubble that will
burst, just as the late-1990s tech bubble led to the Tech Wreck in the early
2000s. Analogies to the credit excesses of the Great Financial Crisis have

### Page 3

also been proffered. Last week's rout in AI-related stocks convinced the
naysayers that the AI bubble is bursting, just as they’ve long predicted it
would.
Our happier spin is that the Bond Vigilantes are now doing a good job of
regulating and allocating capital to the AI ecosystem. Credit-quality
spreads are widening to reflect the riskiness of the bonds issued by some
of the dodgier AI-related borrowers like CoreWeave and Oracle. The
rapidly rising cost of credit for such borrowers should deflate the AI bubble

without bursting it. If some AI-related credits do blow up, the Godfathers of
the AI mafia (along with their protectors in the government) will clean up
the mess. As in "The Godfather II," Michael Corleone has too much at
stake to let his brother Fredo screw up the family business.
Meanwhile, the rout in the AI corporate bond market has triggered a flight
to quality and safety that has stopped the rout in the US Treasury bond
market (chart). This confirms our thesis that the recent rise in US bond

yields mostly reflects better-than-expected economic growth. Tougher
credit conditions in the AI corporate bond market should curb some of the
AI excesses. However, we doubt this will trigger an economy-wide credit
crunch or a recession. We aren't convinced that it will slow capital
spending much, since labor and materials shortages, along with public
NIMBY pushback, have already slowed the pace of data center
construction.
II. S&P Earnings

### Page 4

The big banks reported mixed but solid overall Q3 earnings last week. On
average, they met banking industry analysts' expectations. Last week, the
consensus EPS for all analysts covering S&P 500 companies edged up to
$365.70 this year, $424.19 next year, and $492.62 in 2028 (chart). Those
are extremely bullish expectations considering that we are still forecasting
$500 in 2030! If the analysts are right, the S&P 500 could rise to 10,000
by the end of next year, assuming a forward P/E of 20.0-21.0. For now, we

are sticking with 10,000 by the end of the Roaring 2020s, i.e., at the end
of 2029.
Last week, Q3's consensus EPS growth rate remained at 24.0% y/y, while
the Q4 estimate rose to 29.8% (chart). We've been among the most
bullish investment strategists on the Street, but analysts are still ahead of
us.

### Page 5

The S&P 500, S&P 400, and S&P 600 continued to rise to record highs
last week (chart).
III. Stock Valuation
The recent stock market selloff has been driven by falling forward P/Es
even as forward earnings have been rising to record highs (chart). The
former are nearing the troughs they made during last year's "Liberation
Day" lows.
Since roughly mid-2025, rising FEMO (fabulous earnings momentum) has
more than offset falling FOMO (fear of missing out) (chart). Since October

6, the reverse has been true: Falling FOMO is outpacing the rise in
FEMO.

### Page 6

IV. Performance
The stock market had a tough week last week. Leading the 1.95%
pullback was MAGS (down 3.89%), while XMAGS fell 1.38% (chart). The
Russell 2000 fell 2.4%, while the Nasdaq fell 4.5%. The DJIA rose 0.67%
for the week. The Impressive 493 is still outperforming the Magnificent-7
on a year-to-date basis, with a gain of 14.1% versus 10.2%.
V. Sentiment
The market letter writers have been more bullish than retail investors
lately (chart). Odds are they will both be a bit more pessimistic this week.

### Page 7

Only 47.4% of the S&P 500 are trading above their 200-day moving
averages (chart).
VI. Commodities
Aluminum prices are falling after matching their 2022 peak. This suggests
that supply-chain disruptions attributable to Gulf War III are receding, as
they had done after Russia invaded Ukraine (chart).

### Page 8

Copper prices remained near their recent record high last week despite
chatter that tightening credit conditions could slow the AI buildout and
weigh on overall GDP growth (chart).
The outlook for higher-for-longer diesel prices offers no relief (chart).

### Page 9

We aren't giving up on gold yet. The current correction resembles
previous ones that occurred in the middle of significant gold bull runs
(chart). This time, rising interest rates have been weighing on the metal's
price. However, significant buying by the People's Bank of China and
other central banks isn't interest-rate sensitive. We are still aiming for a
gold price of $5,000 an ounce by the end of this year or early next year.
